### More Pentests Without More Pentesters: Where AI Safely Helps
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-25T15:00:10Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: ai_threat
- Summary: Penetration testers spend a significant portion of their engagements on documentation, reporting, and other tasks outside of testing. PentestPad explains how AI can reduce this workload, help teams handle more engagements, and keep sensitive client data within their own infrastructure. [...]
- Technical details: Penetration testers spend a significant portion of their engagements on documentation, reporting, and other tasks outside of testing. PentestPad explains how AI can reduce this workload, help teams handle more engagements, and keep sensitive client data within their own infrastructure.

Demand for penetration testing keeps rising, be it due to DORA threat-led testing, NIS2, or customer security questionnaires. However, supply often cannot keep up.

Experienced testers are scarce, take months to hire and train, and every one of them spends part of each engagement on work that is not testing. For a company that sells hours, that unbillable part is the margin.

This article shows how to measure it, which parts AI can take over, and how to do that without sending client vulnerabilities to someone else's servers.

Most companies track billable testing time and lack a number for the rest. If you tag the last ten engagements by phase, the picture usually changes:

Two ratios matter: documentation plus review as a share of testing time, and calendar days from last testing day to report delivery. The first sums up to the hidden cost, but only the second is what clients feel.

The tasks that suit a language model are the ones where the tester already knows the answer and needs it written down:

Drafting finding text from tester notes, using your own vulnerability library as the template, so tone and structure stay yours.

Normalizing and de-duplicating scanner output from Burp, Nessus, Nuclei and similar tools into one set of draft findings.

Consistency review: missing remediation, severity that does not match the CVSS vector, the same issue worded three ways.

Executive summary drafts, built from the validated findings and then edited.

It will be helpful to decide beforehand in what capacity to keep humans on severity decisions, exploit validation and anything the client will rely on as a statement of fact.

A model that confidently describes and is set to run an exploit chain that nobody verified can be is worse than no help.

PentestPad covers the whole engagement, from scoping and scanner imports to findings, client delivery and retests.

Its AI can run against a self-hosted LLM inside your own network.

Import scanner output and tester notes into one project as draft findings.

Pull in matching entries from your reusable vulnerability library.

Let the model rewrite each draft in your house style. The tester edits and approves.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text
- EPSS context: EPSS unavailable / not applicable — FIRST.org; not an asset-specific risk score

### The AI Threat Horizon: Why Speed, Edge AI and Baselines Are Our Best Defense
- Source: Palo Alto Unit 42 | Published: 2026-11-25T15:00:09Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: ai_threat
- Summary: Unit 42 examines the evolution of AI-driven cyberattacks and outlines proactive defense strategies for security teams. The post The AI Threat Horizon: Why Speed, Edge AI and Baselines Are Our Best Defense appeared first on Unit 42 .
- Technical details: Unit 42 examines the evolution of AI-driven cyberattacks and outlines proactive defense strategies for security teams.

AI-driven attacks are on the rise, transitioning from theoretical risks to real-world threats. Recent investigations reveal that criminals are using AI to accelerate attacks at scale. We have observed attackers leveraging AI agents to execute context-aware commands with real-time decision-making . In one campaign that we observed, a highly persistent threat actor utilizing AI generated 117 unique scripts across multiple languages and executed over 8,000 unique commands in just 12 days whereas normal attackers usually do dozens of commands with a few scripts for similar time periods.

The behavioral difference between human operators and AI agents is staggering. In one attack we observed, humans acted followed by an agentic AI attacker. In the first (human attacker) phase, time between commands averaged roughly 24 seconds. The hackers made typographical errors, repeated manual checks and demonstrated environment confusion. On the other hand, in the agentic phase of the attack, we observed flawless, zero-syntax-error commands with a median gap of just 1.4 seconds.

When an AI agent is instructed to execute aspects of an attack, it rapidly scans networks, attempts to escalate privileges and utilizes legitimate native tools to blend in. Because of AI’s machine-speed execution, our Mean Time To Respond (MTTR) metrics must improve considerably. Attack isolation and near real-time response are now critical mandates. If we wait hours to react, an AI agent can have already enumerated our cloud infrastructure and extracted or encrypted our data.

To effectively defend against AI-powered attacks, defenders must employ AI-powered tools. This should be done right where the attacks happen. Defenders should also strive to understand the behaviors that separate AI-based attacks from normal operations, whether fully autonomous or partially controlled by a human.

To counter adversaries operating at automated speeds, defense must occur where the attack takes place. Leveraging AI on the edge, specifically on endpoints and Next-Generation Firewalls (NGFW), is a major strategic necessity. Edge-based AI can process telemetry instantly, making split-second decisions to terminate malicious web shell sessions or block active privilege escalation attempts locally.

Edge AI is also exceptionally fast. In our own product deployment, edge AI operated in milliseconds rather than taking minutes like agentic LLMs. It can function independently of cloud access or continuous network connectivity. This capabilities shift is crucial for several key reasons:

Even if an attack vector is used to temporarily disable network traffic to operate silently, local edge AI maintains detection coverage

It enables defenders to catch and isolate threats at the earliest possible stage

It safeguards sensitive corporate data locally without requiring telemetry or proprietary data to be transmitted to the cloud for analysis

Despite their speed, agentic AI attackers have a foundational weakness that affects any attacker: They enter your environment without information. To operate, they must perform extensive reconnaissance, port sweeping and environment enumeration. This is where defenders hold the ultimate upper hand through comprehensive AI analytics.

Winning against machine-speed adversaries requires turning the advantage into real-time detection through automated data fusion and detection. By collecting telemetry across diverse network, endpoint, identity and cloud sources, normalization can map these distinct data streams into a single, cohesive schema. Advanced stitching can then merge related events across these disparate sensors into unified, context-rich stories rather than disconnected events.

Observing these integrated stories allows AI analytics engines to establish baselines of normal organizational behavior across the entire ecosystem. While establishing baselines has been longstanding best practice, AI analytics engines have made it easier for defenders to create these and keep them up to date.

Because threat actors lack this context, their automated attempts to rapidly map the terrain inevitably trigger detectors against established baselines, immediately exposing their presence across our detection layers.

Detection alone was never enough to stop machine-speed threats, but the issue is even worse in the era of AI attacks. Detecting an intrusion minutes after it begins still leaves defenders vulnerable if no immediate action is taken, and in those minutes an agentic AI attacker can cause significant damage to your organization. To truly close the loop against these attacks, organizations must combine real-time detection with automated response capabilities, enabling instant attack isolation and automated remediation before critical systems or data can be compromised.

While the sheer volume and speed of AI attacks can seem daunting, there is a positive takeaway: AI-empowered threat actors are not invisible, and their campaigns are entirely detectable. Every attack that we observed triggered alerts, and deep forensic investigations enabled us to reconstruct the attack lifecycles.

The cybersecurity basics still matter. The tools we have relied on as defenders in the past, including XDR solutions, network monitoring and active prevention rules, are still essential and relevant today. We do not need to throw away our playbooks; we simply need to use them differently. By utilizing our existing tools to automate isolation, leaning into AI on the edge for real-time response and trusting our baseline knowledge of our own environments, we are well-equipped to win against the next generation of threats. Crucially, unifying these tools into a cohesive SOC platform connects the dots across the enterprise — where vertical integration and correct platform usage ultimately drive vastly superior security outcomes.

If there’s one thing to take away from this, it is that Mean Time to Detect and Mean Time to Respond (MTTD and MTTR) are still king. But if we want to win these battles, detection and response times must drop to minutes, not hours.

To safeguard your organization against the unprecedented speed and adaptability of AI-driven threats, now is the time to evaluate your defense strategy. Ensure your enterprise is equipped with advanced detection and response capabilities, deploy robust security technologies across your endpoints, network, cloud and identity environments, and continuously refine their configurations to eliminate coverage gaps. Take action today to empower your security operation with the real-time visibility, automated response, and contextual intelligence needed to stay ahead of next-generation adversaries.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text
- EPSS context: EPSS unavailable / not applicable — FIRST.org; not an asset-specific risk score

### InfraTrust warns of exploited supply chain flaws in network appliances
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-25T15:00:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-109618, CVE-2026-109619, CVE-2026-114625, CVE-2026-123039, CVE-2026-19868, CVE-2026-76418, CVE-2026-76466 | Topic signals: supply_chain
- Summary: Eclypsium's November InfraTrust report warns that most infrastructure security advisories addressing exploited vulnerabilities over the past month involve flaws in third-party software bundled into products rather than code developed by the vendors themselves. [...]
- Technical details: Eclypsium's November InfraTrust report warns that most infrastructure security advisories addressing exploited vulnerabilities over the past month involve flaws in third-party software bundled into products rather than code developed by the vendors themselves.

The November InfraTrust Pulse report tracks 373 new security advisories published by 23 infrastructure vendors between October 15 and November 19, covering 3,880 vulnerabilities.

Of these advisories, 98 were rated critical, and 38 contained vulnerabilities listed in CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog.

However, 33 of those 38 advisories fixed exploited flaws in bundled software components, primarily PHP, Node.js, and OpenSSH.

These vulnerabilities pose additional risks for enterprises, as even after a software developer releases a security fix, organizations must wait for infrastructure vendors to incorporate it into their products.

"Your exposure to exploited flaws depends on how quickly each vendor rebuilds its products after an upstream fix ships," the report reads.

An example of this is the two PHP vulnerabilities known as PHP2Shell (CVE-2026-109618 and CVE-2026-109619) highlighted in last month's InfraTrust report , which are now being exploited in attacks against Palo Alto Networks and Check Point firewalls.

In both cases, threat actors chained the PHP vulnerabilities with other flaws to gain root access to compromised devices, with Check Point reporting destructive attacks that deployed data-wiping malware on VMware ESXi hosts .

As of November 20, PHP2Shell fixes were either partially available or had yet to be released for affected products from Fortinet, Juniper, Lenovo, HPE, Netgear, HP, and Siemens.

The November 19 release of another 18 PHP security advisories further complicated patching efforts for infrastructure vendors and their customers.

With administrators dealing with an increasing number of infrastructure vulnerabilities to address, InfraTrust says to prioritize the following flaws for immediate patching this month:

Cisco Expressway: CVE-2026-76466, a critical vulnerability added to CISA's KEV catalog on November 20. On November 23rd, CISA flagged this flaw as actively exploited after Russian cybersecurity firm F6 spotted hackers using it to gain root privileges.

Palo Alto Networks and Check Point: The PHP2Shell vulnerabilities and CVE-2026-123039 in Check Point gateways. Check Point warned that this flaw was being actively exploited to gain root privileges and establish persistence on vulnerable devices since late October.

Arista VeloCloud Orchestrator: CVE-2026-114625, a critical vulnerability with a CVSS score of 9.9 that was exploited in zero-day attacks .

Cisco Secure Firewall ASA and FTD: CVE-2026-76418, affecting devices configured for remote-access VPNs. The flaw was exploited in attacks to crash devices .

SonicWall SonicOS: CVE-2026-19868, allowing multifactor authentication bypass on VPNs configured with single sign-on. SonicWall warned that it received reports that the flaw was exploited in zero-day attacks .

Administrators should also prioritize installing Fortinet's October hardening updates, which addressed numerous vulnerabilities discovered using AI models.

### Our 2026 Cybersecurity Predictions: What We Got Right, What We Got Wrong, and What Changed
- Source: Rapid7 Research | Published: 2026-11-25T13:48:40Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: ransomware, apt_intrusion, identity, ai_threat
- Summary: Rapid7’s annual Security Predictions webinar returns on December 10, bringing our experts together to explore what the year ahead could hold for cybersecurity. Before sharing our predictions for 2027, we’re grading last year’s calls: that geopolitical tensions would reshape cyber threats, insider threats would dominate breach root causes, and context would become the new currency of cyber performance. Register here for the 2027 Security Predictions webinar . As security leaders work through their priorities for 2027, those predictions offer a useful starting point. The year reinforced several of the concerns we highlighted, while developments in attacker behavior and AI added new dimensions to the discussion. Looking at how those themes unfolded helps us understand what remains relevant and where our thinking needs to evolve. Geopolitics shaped cyber targeting, with businesses caught in 
- Technical details: Rapid7’s annual Security Predictions webinar returns on December 10, bringing our experts together to explore what the year ahead could hold for cybersecurity. Before sharing our predictions for 2027, we’re grading last year’s calls: that geopolitical tensions would reshape cyber threats, insider threats would dominate breach root causes, and context would become the new currency of cyber performance.

Register here for the 2027 Security Predictions webinar .

As security leaders work through their priorities for 2027, those predictions offer a useful starting point. The year reinforced several of the concerns we highlighted, while developments in attacker behavior and AI added new dimensions to the discussion. Looking at how those themes unfolded helps us understand what remains relevant and where our thinking needs to evolve.

Our prediction that geopolitical fault lines would redraw the cyber battlefield earned an A-, as regional conflicts created opportunities for espionage, disruption, and public pressure throughout 2026. Cyber operations allowed adversaries to pursue those objectives below the threshold of open warfare, with commercial organizations exposed alongside government and critical infrastructure targets.

Rapid7 Intelligence tracked a spike in retaliatory activity following regional military escalations in late February. State-directed actors pursued espionage and data theft, while hacktivist personas amplified the impact. Rapid7 also assessed that stolen information was likely to be passed from state actors to hacktivist groups for public release, extending the consequences of an intrusion beyond the initial compromise.

That chain from state actor to hacktivist release makes an organization’s exposure harder to judge from its own location or industry alone. Customers, suppliers, infrastructure, and perceived political associations can attract attention, bringing a seemingly routine commercial relationship into a much wider conflict. Examining those dependencies during response exercises uncovers difficult decisions early, particularly where protecting a service or containing an incident requires agreement across security, operations, legal, and executive leadership.

Our prediction that insider threats would dominate breach root causes drew attention to risks ranging from employee negligence to deliberate access selling. During 2026, reporting on ransomware recruitment attempts reinforced concerns about attackers seeking cooperation from people inside organizations, while the growing use of AI agents broadened the conversation about insider risk. As agents gain access to business systems and take actions on an organization’s behalf, questions about permissions, oversight, and unintended consequences increasingly extend beyond human users.

Our blog on insider recruitment in ransomware campaigns explored an approach by Medusa to a BBC journalist, offering payment in exchange for access. It illustrated how attackers combine financial incentives, persuasion, and pressure to pursue legitimate credentials, giving organizations a concrete reason to include recruitment attempts in awareness programs and response planning.

The wider access economy also remained significant, although credentials appearing for sale do not establish that an employee willingly supplied them. External compromise and exposed systems continued to provide routes into businesses, with AI-assisted vulnerability discovery adding further pressure. Our prediction captured an important concern, while the year reinforced the need to distinguish deliberate insider cooperation from other ways attackers obtain and misuse access.

Our prediction that context would become the new currency of cyber performance earned a B, largely because understanding an environment has always been fundamental to protecting it. During a year shaped by AI-driven vulnerability discovery, however, the ability to connect a new finding with exposed systems, attacker activity, and business consequences became particularly valuable when teams had little time to decide how to respond.

Our analysis of the ASOS incident explored that challenge after an unauthorized notification reached customers through the retailer’s own app. With the route of compromise still unclear, the incident raised questions about access to customer communications platforms and how investigators connect activity across accounts, integrations, and third-party services.

The same need for context applies to remediation, where an advisory alone cannot determine whether interrupting a critical service is justified. Teams need to know where affected technology is running, how attackers could reach it, and which mitigations are practical. Bringing asset information, threat intelligence, and operational knowledge together remains essential to making those decisions with confidence.

Across all three themes, the year reinforced how closely security decisions depend on understanding relationships: between organizations, between identities and access, and between technical weaknesses and business consequences. Those connections will remain relevant as AI changes how technology is used and how threats develop.

Join Rapid7’s Security Predictions webinar on December 10 to explore those shifts with our experts and consider what they could mean for your priorities in the year ahead.

Rapid7s curated database of vulnerabilities, featuring exploit modules and check methods integrated into the Metasploit Framework.

The threat research behind the alerts: adversary tracking, curated intelligence, and flagship threat reports.

Gain 24x7 XDR monitoring, remediation, and DFIR from experts that extend your team to help secure your extended ecosystem.

For details on how to apply this update, which includes the changes described in this advisory, refer to:

The system must be rebooted for this update to take effect.

Red Hat recommends treating all kernel errata as security-relevant. Given the kernel's fundamental role, any bug has a higher chance of impacting system security, even if that impact only becomes clear after a fix is published. Therefore, Red Hat prioritizes delivering fixes that improve our customers' overall security posture.

Because of this proactive approach, a patch may be associated with a CVE assignment at a future date. Retroactive CVE assignments are always documented in the corresponding errata and on Red Hat's CVE pages. We strongly advise against delaying updates, as doing so may leave your system exposed when protections are already available.

### Hackers Exploit Two GeoServer Flaws as Researchers Warn of Unauthenticated RCE Chains
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-25T12:48:54Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-120704, CVE-2026-120705, CVE-2026-129243, CVE-2026-129244, CVE-2026-129245 | Topic signals: apt_intrusion
- Summary: Threat actors are actively exploiting two newly patched security flaws in GeoServer, according to watchTowr. The vulnerabilities in question are CVE-2026-120705 and CVE-2026-129243, details of which are below - CVE-2026-120705 (CVSS score: 8.6) - An arbitrary file read vulnerability that could enable an attacker to access local text files readable by the GeoServer process or fetch
- Technical details: Attackers are exploiting two newly patched GeoServer flaws that could enable file disclosure and authorization bypass without authentication.

Threat actors are actively exploiting two newly patched security flaws in GeoServer, according to watchTowr .

The vulnerabilities in question are CVE-2026-120705 and CVE-2026-129243, details of which are below -

CVE-2026-120705 (CVSS score: 8.6) - An arbitrary file read vulnerability that could enable an attacker to access local text files readable by the GeoServer process or fetch adversary-selected HTTP or HTTPS URLs by means of a single unauthenticated Web Feature Service (WFS) request.

CVE-2026-129243 (CVSS score: 8.8) - An incorrect authorization vulnerability that could allow an unauthenticated attacker to invoke OWS operations that are otherwise protected by service security rules.

Advisories for both the flaws were published on November 24, 2026, in the following versions -

CVE-2026-120705: org.geoserver:gs-wfs-core - 3.0.2, 2.28.6, and 2.27.7

CVE-2026-120705: org.geotools:gt-metadata - 35.2, 34.6, and 33.7

CVE-2026-129243: org.geoserver:gs-ows - 3.0.2, 2.28.6, and 2.27.7

"Attackers sent error-based exploit probes to validate that the server is vulnerable by creating and invoking stored queries," the preemptive exposure management firm said in an X post today.

Yordan Ganchev, principal threat intelligence specialist at watchTowr, said the vulnerabilities can allow attackers to bypass security restrictions, interact with internal services, retrieve stored secrets, forge authentication credentials, and deserialize and run code on affected systems.

"On November 25, we observed in-the-wild exploitation against our global honeypot network in the early morning hours leveraging CVE-2026-120705 and CVE-2026-129244," Ganchev said. "Threat actors from multiple IP addresses were observed validating vulnerable systems on the internet by creating stored queries on GeoServer machines, checking whether they were created, and deleting them immediately afterward."

Eye Security, which discovered and reported CVE-2026-120705, said the flaw can be combined with two other vulnerabilities in the same product to create an unauthenticated remote code execution chain -

CVE-2026-129245 (CVSS score: 9.2) - A use of a hard-coded signing key ("geoserver") vulnerability in the "remember me" functionality that could be exploited to forge administrator authentication. (Fixed in org.geoserver:gs-main versions 3.0.2, 2.28.6, and 2.27.7)

CVE-2026-120704 (CVSS score: 7.2) - An improper control of the generation of code vulnerability that could allow an administrator, or anyone with access to an administrator session, to execute arbitrary code as the user running GeoServer. (Fixed in org.geoserver.security:gs-sec-jdbc versions 3.0.2, 2.28.6, and 2.27.7)

In a hypothetical attack chain, a bad actor can follow the steps below -

Exploit CVE-2026-120705 to access "users.xml," a file containing GeoServer usernames and password hashes, and obtain the password representation along with the static signing key extracted from the security configuration ("security/config.xml").

Exploit CVE-2026-129245 to forge a valid administrator authentication cookie by using the predictable signing key and the stored password representation without having to know the administrator password.

Exploit CVE-2026-120704 to abuse an administrator-controlled JDBC configuration to execute code from an uploaded native library on both Linux and Windows versions of GeoServer.
- Fix / remediation: (Fixed in org.geoserver:gs-main versions 3.0.2, 2.28.6, and 2.27.7) (Fixed in org.geoserver.security:gs-sec-jdbc versions 3.0.2, 2.28.6, and 2.27.7)

### U.S. Treasury's Financial Crimes Agency Takes Some Systems Offline After Cyber Intrusion
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-25T12:30:08Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: apt_intrusion
- Summary: The U.S. Treasury Department's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN) has been hit by a cybersecurity incident that resulted in "unauthorized access to a limited subset of its systems," the bureau said on Tuesday. As a precaution, it has taken some systems offline. The bureau collects suspicious activity reports and other Bank Secrecy Act (BSA) filings from banks and other financial
- Technical details: U.S. Treasury's FinCEN confirms unauthorized access to a limited subset of its systems and takes some systems offline as a precaution.

The U.S. Treasury Department's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network ( FinCEN ) has been hit by a cybersecurity incident that resulted in "unauthorized access to a limited subset of its systems," the bureau said on Tuesday. As a precaution, it has taken some systems offline.

The bureau collects suspicious activity reports and other Bank Secrecy Act (BSA) filings from banks and other financial institutions and analyzes them to fight money laundering and terrorist financing.

The BSA E-Filing website is still up, although some services may be temporarily unavailable. The agency has not named the systems it reached or those it took offline.

To support filers affected by the disruption, the bureau is extending some filing deadlines: covered reports originally due between November 18 and December 29 can now be submitted until December 30.

A relief order issued the same day, FIN-2026-R002, applies to three groups:

Financial institutions that used Secure Direct Transfer Mode (SDTM), the channel for sending batches of BSA reports directly from their own servers, as of November 18

Companies required to file a beneficial ownership information report during the covered window

Individuals required to update a FinCEN Identifier during the same window

All other Bank Secrecy Act obligations stay in force, including the duty to monitor for suspicious transactions. The service alert says urgent suspicious activity information can still reach law enforcement through the Financial Institutions Hotline, 1-866-556-3974, which is not affected by the disruption.

The disclosure followed days of filing trouble. Filing providers reported failures in automated batch submissions beginning November 18, and the bureau posted a notice about the disruption to SDTM late last week, according to a filing outages report by the markets news site The Deep Dive.

The bureau has not said whether the intrusion caused those outages. The order grants the relief because of a "service disruption" and does not mention the cybersecurity incident.

Nor has the agency said when the access began, whether any data was viewed or taken, or who was responsible. No group has publicly claimed the intrusion so far. Those answers matter because of what the bureau holds: reports of suspicious financial activity filed by financial institutions nationwide.

The incident is not Treasury's first in recent years: in December 2024, a China-linked group used a compromised BeyondTrust API key in an earlier Treasury breach that reached department systems and documents. No link between the two incidents has been reported.

- Technical details: On November 24, 2026, Broadcom released VMware Security Advisory VMSA-2026-0011 to address several new vulnerabilities, including CVE-2026-123903, a critical authentication bypass in the VMware Directory Service component of vCenter. Broadcom assigned it a CVSSv3.1 score of 9.8 Critical. An attacker who can reach an affected vCenter’s VMware Directory Service (vmdir) over the network can gain access without authenticating.

On November 24, 2026, Broadcom released VMware Security Advisory VMSA-2026-0011 to address several new vulnerabilities, including CVE-2026-123903 , a critical authentication bypass in the VMware Directory Service component of vCenter. Broadcom assigned it a CVSSv3.1 score of 9.8 Critical . An attacker who can reach an affected vCenter’s VMware Directory Service (vmdir) over the network can gain access without authenticating.

Broadcom's supplemental FAQ distinguishes CVE-2026-123903 from an older vulnerability CVE-2026-123890 , which Rapid7 covered previously when CVE-2026-123890 was reported as being exploited in the wild. Both of these vulnerabilities affect the VMware Directory Service in vCenter and can give an attacker access to the vCenter without credentials. CVE-2026-123890 was reachable over RPC while CVE-2026-123903 is reachable over LDAP.

As of this writing, there are no known public reports of exploitation of CVE-2026-123903 in the wild. A public proof-of-concept exploit is available and has been validated by Rapid7 to successfully achieve an authentication bypass.

Attackers are known to be targeting vulnerabilities in the VMware Directory Service, therefore Rapid7 recommends remediating exposed systems on an urgent basis outside of normal patching cycles.

Vendor updates are available for CVE-2026-123903. Broadcom lists no workaround , and these vCenter updates are not eligible for Quick Patch. Rapid7 recommends updating affected deployments on an urgent basis, outside normal patch cycles, using the standard update mechanism for the deployment.

Broadcom lists the following available fixed versions to remediating CVE-2026-123903. These fixed versions also remediate several other high and medium severity CVEs from the same advisory (CVE-2026-123904, CVE-2026-123905, CVE-2026-123906, CVE-2026-123907, CVE-2026-123908, CVE-2026-123909):

Note that if vCenter 8.0 U3m was previously installed when remediating CVE-2026-123890, it also fixes CVE-2026-123903 and no further vCenter update is needed.

For the latest affected-version and remediation guidance, refer to Broadcom's security advisory and supplemental FAQ .

Exposure Command, Vulnerability Management (InsightVM), and Nexpose customers can assess exposure to CVE-2026-123903 on VMware vCenter Server with an unauthenticated vulnerability check expected to be available in the November 25, 2026 content release.

Customers leveraging Rapid7’s Intelligence Hub can access Suricata detection rules for detection of the public CVE-2026-123903 exploit.

These types of vulnerabilities are a frequent attack vector for malicious cyber actors and pose significant risks to the federal enterprise.

Binding Operational Directive (BOD) 26-04: Prioritizing Security Updates Based on Risk establishes vulnerability management requirements for Federal Civilian Executive Branch (FCEB) agencies. BOD 26-04 reinforces the importance of the KEV Catalog and requires federal agencies to prioritize rapid remediation of high-risk vulnerabilities, specifically those identified by Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVEs) listed in CISA’s KEV Catalog on publicly exposed assets that grant total control of the asset post-exploitation, while deferring action for lower-risk vulnerabilities. BOD 26-04 further establishes basic expectations for when agencies must check whether threat actors compromised the system before the patch was applied.

While BOD 26-04 applies only to FCEB agencies, CISA encourages all organizations to adopt risk-based vulnerability management and prioritize remediation of KEV Catalog vulnerabilities . CISA will continue to add vulnerabilities to the catalog that meet the specified criteria .

Aware of an exploited vulnerability not currently listed in the KEV Catalog? Submit it for potential addition through CISA’s KEV Nomination Form . Potential KEV additions must have a CVE ID, evidence of exploitation, and clear mitigation guidance.

This product is provided subject to this Notification and this Privacy & Use policy.

### Credential Remediation Is Slow When You Don’t Know What Will Break
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-25T11:51:48Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: identity
- Summary: TL;DR More than 64% still valid after four years: GitGuardian’s State of Secrets Sprawl 2026 found that most valid secrets discovered in 2022 still worked in January 2026, because teams stall on what a revoke will break. Validity-only triage misses 46% of critical secrets: Prioritize by impact instead, weighing what a credential can access and whether it leaked publicly, and update the
- Technical details: GitGuardian finds 64% of valid secrets discovered in 2022 still worked in January 2026, while validity-only triage misses 46% of critical secrets.

More than 64% still valid after four years: GitGuardian’s State of Secrets Sprawl 2026 found that most valid secrets discovered in 2022 still worked in January 2026, because teams stall on what a revoke will break.

Validity-only triage misses 46% of critical secrets: Prioritize by impact instead, weighing what a credential can access and whether it leaked publicly, and update the risk score as new context arrives.

Revoke last, never first: Issue a replacement, store it in the vault, switch every consumer to the vault call, deploy and monitor, then revoke the original and confirm it no longer authenticates.

Every modern enterprise depends on credentials. This is how humans, systems, and now AI connect securely to data, services, and each other. GitGuardian helps secure that credential layer through three connected capabilities: Detect, Remediate, and Prevent. The journey starts with detection, but credential remediation is where many secret security programs bog down. This is the second of three articles we are releasing to explain how we help address detection, remediation, and prevention.

The first article in this series, “ The Credential Layer Is Expanding Faster Than Security Teams Can See It ,” covered detection across every surface where credentials live. Just knowing they exist is not enough. Teams need to take action to remediate those findings. To do that, they need context around every credential they find.

Without the right context, it is impossible to know what risks the secret carries. It is also difficult to understand which systems rely on those secrets and the risk levels that revoking the secret brings. Nobody wants to break production, but we also cannot allow attackers to leverage those secrets.

The resulting decision paralysis leaves many teams struggling to rotate immediately. Sometimes it can take years for the secret to be revoked finally. That is actually all too common, as shown by GitGuardian’s State of Secrets Sprawl 2026 research that found that 64% of valid secrets discovered in 2022 still worked in January 2026, a full 4 years later.

It would be great if there were a one-size-fits-all solution for remediating every leak; in reality, each one is tied to a specific integration. What we can do is streamline the processes needed to get to the end, making it much easier for organizations of any size. And it does take the whole organization to properly deal with leaked secrets.

A team that discovers an exposed credential rarely has all the authority or knowledge needed to fix it without causing further issues. A security engineer can see it’s valid and dangerous, but that security engineer usually cannot immediately tell which services will fail when it is revoked. Breaking production is considered a much higher risk than the possibility of a stolen and abused secret, so they need to go looking for answers, which is a time-consuming process.

The developers who know where and why a key is used and, hopefully, how to rotate it safely are often busy shipping new features and managing existing requests from the business. An alert in their email that shows only a file path and a secret type asks those busy devs to stop their workflow and launch their own investigation. These requests, all too often, sink into the backlog.

At the same time, the larger the organization, the harder it is to find the right person who knows the answer to “what breaks if this key is revoked?” Application security teams monitor code where keys are used, identity and access management (IAM) teams govern identities that were issued keys, and the Platform Engineering team runs the vaults holding the keys. They all have different perspectives and, in most organizations that are early in their secrets security maturity journey , can only tell part of the story.

Endpoint security teams are also watching what happens at the workstation level. They are beginning to track secrets stored there, as software supply chain attacks have increasingly targeted these plaintext credential stores over the last couple of years. A secret leaked here might even be the triggering event for the rotation in case of compromise from an infostealer. The endpoint team is historically not connected to AppSec or Platform teams.

One credential can touch all these teams while each individual, isolated group sees only its own slice of the credential layer. Now, multiply the communication and coordination issue across hundreds or thousands of potential leaks, and the scale of the remediation problem becomes clearer.

The risk of revoking before fully understanding the fallout is fairly high for the business. This is weighed against potential future abuse. Of course, this math changes during an active breach when the clock is ticking and when exfiltration or lateral movement is already occurring. Those emergencies do not get backlogged. Mature organizations understand that addressing leaks before an emergency arises is the better plan overall, but remediation times are still measured in weeks for most teams. How long a remediation process takes really boils down to how fast and accurately responders can gather and communicate information about the issue. Responders need to know a lot of things, starting with whether the credential works, what it can access, and who owns it before they can even ask questions like “where else does it appear?” and “which systems depend on it?”

For example, envision two valid cloud credentials. One grants read-only access to a single storage bucket, and the second one is a root-level key with administrative control over production. While their privileges and dependent workloads determine the appropriate level of urgency, both require the same level of insight and investigation to triage the remediation process and decide whether the response is a rotation next sprint or a revocation this very second.

The ultimate goal is to remove the leaked key rapidly, but in a way that does not interrupt production or break pipeline builds. Rotating secrets safely simply requires resources and a coordination plan. If this process is always piecemeal and manual, then mistakes are more likely to happen.

A security team that causes an outage once will face resistance the next time it asks to rotate anything.

Validity is the first and clearest signal most responders look at. Alone, this is an incomplete signal. GitGuardian research from the State of Secrets report found that validity-only triage misses 46% of critical secrets such as custom API tokens and SSH keys, because those secrets cannot be verified automatically.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

### Drupal Fail2Install Exploit Details Emerge as Critical RCE Flaw Faces Active Exploitation
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-25T11:23:26Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-132165 | Topic signals: active_exploitation
- Summary: The U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) on Tuesday added a critical flaw impacting Drupal to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog, requiring federal agencies to apply the fixes by November 27, 2026. The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-132165 aka Fail2Install (CVSS score: 9.8), is a case of remote code execution (RCE) impacting Drupal core. "Drupal Core
- Technical details: Drupal Fail2Install Exploit Details Emerge as Critical RCE Flaw Faces Active Exploitation | Read more hacking news on The Hacker News cybersecurity news website and learn how to protect against cyberattacks and software vulnerabilities.

The U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) on Tuesday added a critical flaw impacting Drupal to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities ( KEV ) catalog, requiring federal agencies to apply the fixes by November 27, 2026.

The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-132165 aka Fail2Install (CVSS score: 9.8), is a case of remote code execution (RCE) impacting Drupal core.

"Drupal Core contains a not failing securely vulnerability that could allow an unauthenticated attacker to take over an existing site or execute arbitrary code via the Drupal installer," CISA said. "Under specific conditions, the attacker can use install.php to attempt to reinstall Drupal on the site."

As previously reported by The Hacker News, an attacker can use the installer to attempt to reinstall Drupal, resulting in site takeover or remote code execution under certain circumstances.

According to an independent analysis by Searchlight Cyber, it has emerged that ordinary server resource limits can be abused to trick Drupal into mistakenly believing that an existing website was not installed, enter its installation process via "install.php," create an administrator-controlled account, and execute arbitrary PHP code.

While Drupal has checks in place to determine whether the website is already installed before allowing installation to proceed, the research found that it's possible to bypass these checks by exhausting server resources.

As a result, a failed database connection to the configured database and a failed database check for an existing installation are interpreted as evidence that the website has not been installed, thereby opening the door to a scenario where Drupal allows the installation to proceed but only after winning a race condition that allows a second database connectivity check to pass.

"So if the database refuses the early connections and accepts the later one, all within one request, the installer concludes the site is new and shows its setup forms on a live site," StepSecurity said in an explainer. "

"The installer's database form saves the settings it receives to settings.php. Once an attacker submits that form with their own database settings, the live site points at the attacker's database. The attacker then creates the site's administrator account."

To achieve this, the three resource exhaustion techniques have been put to use -

EMFILE , which refers to a limit on how many files a Linux process can open

"We started with EMFILE, then explored database and TCP limits to determine whether availability failures could become security primitives," the cybersecurity company said.

"Applying this methodology to Drupal uncovered a pre-authentication RCE affecting Drupal 8.9, 9, 10, and 11 across four common deployment combinations: Apache with PostgreSQL, Apache with MySQL, PHP-FPM with PostgreSQL, and PHP-FPM with MySQL."

This behavior can be turned into a pathway for code execution by taking advantage of the fact that Drupal's installer supports SQLite database files and allows the database path to be selected during installation. This is possible because the installer does not enforce restrictions on the file name extension.

"By selecting a path ending in .php under the document root, the installer creates an SQLite database with a PHP extension," Searchlight Cyber said. "Once the installation reaches the site configuration form, Drupal stores the submitted website name inside the SQLite database. By using PHP code as the website name, the payload bytes are written into the .php database file."

While direct access to the generated PHP file is blocked by Drupal's stock .htaccess on a stock Apache deployment, a path-handling weakness in the Apache RewriteRule could be exploited to achieve arbitrary PHP execution.

"Starting from three system-wide limits, we showed how errors that appear to affect only availability can become primitives for crossing a security boundary," Searchlight Cyber researchers Kevin Gervot and Adam Kues said.

"In Drupal, temporary database failures can produce a FAIL, FAIL, and then SUCCESS sequence that reopens the installer on an existing website and ultimately leads to pre-authentication RCE through an SQLite database containing PHP code."
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

### Microsoft fixes Microsoft 365 bug blocking access to Office apps
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-25T11:16:32Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: Earlier this week, Microsoft resolved a known issue that was preventing some Microsoft 365 customers from accessing Office applications. [...]
- Technical details: Earlier this week, Microsoft resolved a known issue that was preventing some Microsoft 365 customers from accessing Office applications.

This follows widespread reports on social media and Microsoft's support website about what affected users described as a persistent white screen or square that can't be dismissed, displayed over Office apps' user interfaces or Microsoft 365 sign-in windows.

"We've installed 3 separate versions of 365 on this device, but no matter how we try to get into a 365 app we get stuck on this white screen. I've launched applications in safe mode as well without any luck," one user said.

The company confirmed in a support document updated Monday that a blank dialog box prevented customers from opening Microsoft 365 Outlook, Word, Excel, PowerPoint, OneNote, and Access apps.

"When you open an Office application, a blank dialog window might appear. The dialog box doesn't show any text or buttons," Microsoft said . "If you can't dismiss it, you might be unable to continue using the application."

Microsoft says the issue is now fixed in Microsoft 365 Apps Version 2611 (Build 16.0.20625.20142), released last week .

​However, Microsoft says that customers who can't update because of this bug can also upgrade to the latest version from the command line by going through the following procedure:

Run the following command "%programfiles%\Common Files\Microsoft Shared\ClickToRun\officec2rclient.exe" /update user updatetoversion=16.0.20625.20142 .

This is the last in a series of bugs that have affected Microsoft 365 and Office customers since the start of the year.

For instance, in June, Microsoft fixed another issue that blocked Office for the web users from opening Excel and PowerPoint documents , and another that prevented Windows 365 users from downloading the Office suite.

One month later, it resolved a bug that blocked third-party apps from launching Microsoft Office apps like Word, Excel, PowerPoint, and Access and from opening documents on Windows systems.

More recently, in September, Microsoft fixed a known issue causing copy-and-paste and autofill failures for Excel users after installing the September 2026 security updates.

That same month, Microsoft also had to temporarily pause the rollout of the KB5002907 Microsoft 365 update to fix a bug that deactivated (and in some cases completely removed) perpetual Office 2016 and Office 2019 installations for some users.

### The Great Escape: Tradecraft insights from an agentic swarm
- Source: Cisco Talos | Published: 2026-11-25T11:00:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: active_exploitation, ai_threat
- Summary: The OpenAI-linked swarm activity in 2026 is fully traceable through public records. This blog analyzes its tradecraft and compares it to early signals and behaviors from current agentic attack activity seen in the wild.
- Technical details: The OpenAI-linked swarm activity in 2026 is fully traceable through public records. This blog analyzes its tradecraft and compares it to early signals and behaviors from current agentic attack activity seen in the wild.

The OpenAI-linked swarm activity in 2026 is fully traceable through public records and offers an unprecedented view into how multi-agent AI systems operate and coordinate at scale.

Distilling and documenting the swarm tradecraft may help defenders understand multi-agent operations and communication, and how agents adapt when a path is blocked.

Some documented behaviors may transfer to more purpose-built cyber attack frameworks like ARTEX, including methods to persistently store results, the burst-and-pause timing patterns of reasoning loops, and the self-labeling conventions that can reveal workload assignments and distribution.

Beginning in June 2026 and extending for several months, a collection of OpenAI models conducting internal training and evaluation tasks broke out of their containerized environments and accessed websites and databases via the public internet in ways the company says it did not authorize .

This activity represents a new kind of cyber event: not a malicious intrusion, but an unintentional series of consequences stemming from an agentic swarm , a group of AI agents exhibiting coordinated behavior toward a shared objective. A swarm implies many agents but doesn’t have a rigid count. The main distinction is behavioral; the collective activity of a swarm becomes complex and unpredictable even when individual agents may be explainable. This swarm provided an initial, public glimpse into the behavior and coordination of autonomous, multi-agent operations.

Due to constraints in their evaluation environment, the OpenAI swarm used public internet services as improvised infrastructure, which made the activity visible and traceable. The behaviors exposed revealed methods for persistent probing, iterative payload development, work partitioning and coordination across agents, and adaptation to execution constraints, in ways that are likely to be relevant for understanding agent behavior beyond this just case.

Agentic cyber attack frameworks are now appearing in intrusion activity , with the ability to support scaling to multi-agent operations . These attacker swarms largely replace the use of public internet resources with purpose-built infrastructure that removes some of the artifacts visible. However, documenting our understanding of operational swarm techniques can give defenders a baseline for recognizing behaviors as they move into new environments and contexts. In some cases, we can already see the evidence where these techniques do transfer.

In this post, we’ll explore and document the tradecraft of the escaped OpenAI swarm and conclude by comparing it to early signals and behaviors from the current agentic attack activity seen in the wild.

Swarms are an important concept to track because they can divide work across parallel agents, potentially completing complex tasks faster, though the gains depend on the task and the quality of coordination. Parallel agent coordination was crucial to OpenAI’s ability to solve the Navier-Stokes Millenium Prize Problem, which arrived at a winning solution with a group on the order of 10,000 concurrent agents over the course of 88 hours.

Early evidence suggests swarms transfer effectively to cyber attack problems. The main factors for swarm efficiency are how parallelizable the tasking is, and if the attackers want to pay the inflated token cost. A rough example is using four agents on the same problem, to get it done twice as fast; as Noam Brown of OpenAI said on a Dwarkesh podcast , “Because there are four agents working for half as long, you’re paying 2x more to get an answer twice as quickly.”

AI models undergoing training and evaluation operate inside sandboxed computing environments. These are controlled workspaces where they can write and run code, search for information, and iterate toward solutions. These environments are designed to be both enabling and constraining. They give models room to practice, while limiting what they can reach and do, so that mistakes or misaligned behavior stay contained.

While the techniques employed by the OpenAI swarm may have crossed the thresholds of intended use, they should not be viewed as malicious intent. A useful framing for understanding these techniques is creativity under constraint. The agents were optimized to meet their objectives, and they displayed considerable persistence and ingenuity to that end.

The constraints the agents were working around were layered and understanding them helps explain the motivation behind every technique. The most fundamental was the absence of a browser runtime: Agents operating in text and code environments cannot natively load JavaScript-heavy pages, call browser APIs, or interact with dynamic web content. The creative solution was submitting URLs to a public scanner like “urlquery.net” which provided a real Chromium instance on demand.

This workaround by itself, however, was still not enough. Browsers apply a security rule called the same-origin policy: JavaScript running on one website generally cannot read data from another. Cross-origin resource sharing (CORS) lets a destination website explicitly permit access. The agents used public CORS proxies to relay requests when target websites did not grant permission.

They also lacked infrastructure of their own to host payloads or receive data, so they relied on public services such as httpbin, httpbun, and "webhook.site" services capable of testing HTTP requests and responses but not designed for this activity.

In some evaluation environments another constraint applied: Agents appear to have been restricted to GET requests only, with no ability to make outbound POST calls. That is likely what pushed them toward using public wikis as shared state stores , as wiki page edits are a write operation achievable through ordinary browser navigation.

In response to their constraints and in persistent pursuit of their objectives, the swarm developed and refined its tradecraft over time. This included chaining legitimate web services like URL shorteners, CORS proxies (services that relay requests to bypass browser security restrictions), Base64 decode endpoints (services that decode encoded data back into executable web pages), and markdown renderers into extraction pipelines that never required dedicated attacker infrastructure.

Talos observed structured work partitioning across parallel agent instances, iterative probe development, and sandbox identification techniques. The agents coordinated using a variety of identifying tags (e.g., uqscan=claude... , CHATGPTTEST1 , OAI_META_1312 , archive_test=20261004 ). These self-labels, intended as internal tracking strings, became the primary cross-campaign fingerprint linking multiple disparate operations.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

### Securing Grandma's groceries with Pierre Cadieux
- Source: Cisco Talos | Published: 2026-11-25T11:00:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: Pierre takes us back to his early days on help desks in the 90s, where learning to correlate logs between Windows and Unix systems sparked the curiosity that eventually led him to security.
- Technical details: Pierre takes us back to his early days on help desks in the 90s, where learning to correlate logs between Windows and Unix systems sparked the curiosity that eventually led him to security.

This month, Amy is joined by Pierre Cadieux, Research Lead for Intelligence Efficacy at Talos. Pierre takes us back to his early days on help desks in the 90s, where learning to correlate logs between Windows and Unix systems sparked the curiosity that eventually led him to security. It’s a great look at how someone who has seen the industry evolve over decades stays grounded and keeps their edge. We also get into what’s kept Pierre at Cisco for nearly ten years. He talks about why he prioritizes the human impact of our work, the importance of knowing when to step away from the keyboard, and how his background in anthropology actually informs the way he approaches threat intelligence today.

Amy Ciminnisi: I've known you since you were in Talos IR, which was not too long ago. But tell me a little bit about your professional journey, because I know that definitely was not the start of it.

Pierre Cadieux: I worked at an ISP as part of the help desk back in the mid-90s. I was one of the people who people would call up and say, "Hey, I've got this computer I just bought, how do I get it on the internet?" I did a lot of learning about what TCP/IP was, what networking was, how to connect things via various different types of connections — whether it be an actual hard-coded network connection or a dial-up connection — and was able to get some expertise around guiding people through that process.

Then I moved to a technology company in San Diego and worked on their help desk. One day, someone called me up and said, "You know how to read Linux logs, right? Or Unix logs, UCS logs, Linux logs." I said, "Yeah." They said, "You know how to use Windows — you're a Windows Server guy, too." I said, "Yeah." They said, "I need you to sit in this chair, look at these two screens — one is a Windows box, one is a Unix box. I need you to be a correlation engine."

So, I looked at the timestamps for both of them, and helped to figure out what was happening because we were having an emergency, an incident, and they had nobody that could look at both. People at that point in time in this organization were very much, "I am doing just Unix things and only know Unix things. I am Windows things only. I only know one of those things." They didn't cross the streams. I could talk both. So I sat in the chair, I stared at both screens and I helped them to track the timeline of what was happening, which ended up being an intrusion exploiting a vulnerable SSH server that was compromised. That changed my course into security.

I spent a number of years in internal security for a number of organizations and got into leadership positions, even CISO positions, leading security teams globally for financial services, for instance, and others. Then I moved into consulting and did that for a number of years as well.

AC: Now you are on the TI&I team, and you also just started contributing to our Threat Source newsletter. What is it that kept you here after all those years?

PC: I'm hitting my ten-year anniversary at Cisco in January. What keeps me at Cisco and Talos is the flexibility of my work. I'm able to, within most bounds, choose what I work on and how I get things done. I have a lot of flexibility in my workday, day-to-day. I work West Coast hours compared to most of my team members who are East Coast. So I sometimes work early shifts with them and am done by the early afternoon. Or sometimes I have to work with folks who are over in Asia Pacific, and I'm working later.

The mission that Talos has resonates with me deeply. One of the things that I have seen along the way was not forgetting the why behind security, not forgetting the fact that what we are defending matters to people, not just to corporations, but to people who are affected by these things. In a newsletter intro, I mentioned that at one of the places I worked, they explained to me that a certain machine was super critical because it wrote the checks that get sent out to people who are expecting this check to come in weekly, monthly, whatever — that was going to be paying for grandma's groceries. And so that motivated me to make sure that box never went down and protect it in a very specific way. Even though it was something that probably should have been sunset and wasn't necessarily up to snuff from a security perspective, there are things we could do to protect it that would allow it to still function as we look for a better solution. At Talos, leadership gives us the space to execute that mission in a way that makes sense for us.

Want to see more? Watch the full interview , and don’t forget to subscribe to our YouTube channel for future episodes of Humans of Talos.

What does it mean to be "blue collar" for the blue team? Ryan Fetterman discusses the realities of detection engineering, and shares his philosophy on building a career in security without burning out.

Amy looks back at the incredible journeys that brought past guests to the world of threat intelligence.

Ever wonder how someone goes from studying human viruses to leading cybersecurity teams? In this Humans of Talos, we’re joined by Martin Lee, EMEA Lead, to talk about his journey into the industry.

- Technical details: A former OpenAI employee accused of bugging company offices was granted $1 million bond after investigators found hundreds of audio files.

A former OpenAI employee accused of concealing a covert recording device at the company's San Francisco offices will be allowed to be released from jail on a $1 million bond, a San Francisco federal court judge ruled Tuesday.

Abdulrahman Alfozan, a 35-year-old Saudi national, was arrested on October 9, 2026, for allegedly bugging the artificial intelligence (AI) company's offices with the aim of recording sensitive conversations.

Subsequent court filings have pointed out that Alfozan was in touch on WhatsApp with a member of the Saudi royal family, as well as expressed interest in moving back to Saudi Arabia following OpenAI's initial public offering (IPO).

He has been charged with using or endeavoring to use an electronic, mechanical, or other device to intercept oral communications. If convicted, Alfozan faces a maximum penalty of 5 years in prison and a $250,000 fine, followed by 3 years of supervised release.

"At this time, we have no indications of this individual inappropriately accessing or exfiltrating model weights, source code, or customer data," the ChatGPT-maker said at the time.

Alfozan pleaded not guilty to the charges during his appearance in San Francisco federal court earlier this month.

According to CNBC , the decision comes after prosecutors told the court last week that they had "found no new evidence to support the theory that Mr. Alfozan was stealing trade secrets, or to support the speculation that he was acting as an unregistered foreign agent for Saudi Arabia."

However, they said investigators have found hundreds of audio files on the defendant's personal devices, including dozens of recordings of meetings or conversations that took place at OpenAI throughout September 2026.

Some of these involved meetings with OpenAI customers and contained material protected by attorney-client privilege, prosecutors said, adding he possessed a photograph of what's believed to be a term sheet for a "strategic partnership" between OpenAI and another company.

"What the defendant did or intended to do with those audio files remains under investigation," they said.

Under the bail conditions, Alfozan will be required to wear a GPS ankle monitor, observe a curfew from 7 p.m. to 7 a.m., stay within the Northern District of California, and avoid airports and train and bus stations. He is expected to be released on Wednesday morning.

"There is no spy story that we're talking about right now," U.S. Magistrate Judge Alex G. Tse said, per The Wall Street Journal . "The government doesn't have that."

### Hackers now exploit critical Drupal RCE flaw to hijack sites
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-25T10:10:20Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-132165 | Topic signals: active_exploitation
- Summary: The Drupal security team has confirmed that a critical vulnerability that lets attackers hijack unpatched sites has been actively exploited since at least Thursday. [...]
- Technical details: The Drupal security team has confirmed that a critical vulnerability that lets attackers hijack unpatched sites has been actively exploited since at least Thursday.

Drupal is a content management system (CMS) commonly used by large organizations, including government entities, major research universities, and high-profile enterprise and media organizations.

The security flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-132165, was discovered by Anthropic's OSS Scanner and can be exploited by anonymous users to gain remote code execution or take over vulnerable sites by abusing the Drupal installer.

"Drupal core ships with an install.php file for installing Drupal core. Under specific conditions, an attacker can use the installer to attempt to reinstall Drupal. This can lead to a site takeover or remote code execution," the Drupal security team warned last Wednesday. "Some exploit paths are mitigated if the web server cannot write to or change permissions on settings.php."

While it wasn't initially flagged as abused in the wild, in an update to the original security advisory, Drupal warned that this flaw has been "exploited on live sites as early as 2026-11-19 19:00 UTC."

Websites using the paid Drupal Steward Web Application Firewall (WAF) service are already protected from known attack vectors, but admins are still advised to upgrade to the latest available Drupal version in case other attack vectors are discovered.

Those who can't immediately upgrade should block access to /core/install.php in the web server configuration or the .htaccess file in the web root to secure the Drupal installer.

On Tuesday, the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) has also added the vulnerability to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) Catalog and ordered federal agencies to secure their systems against ongoing attacks by Friday, November 27.

"These types of vulnerabilities are a frequent attack vector for malicious cyber actors and pose significant risks to the federal enterprise," the agency warned. "While BOD 26-04 applies only to FCEB agencies, CISA encourages all organizations to adopt risk-based vulnerability management and prioritize remediation of KEV Catalog vulnerabilities."

Since November 2021, CISA has tagged five Drupal vulnerabilities as abused in the wild, two of which have also been exploited by ransomware groups.

The SMB/CIFS protocol is a standard file sharing protocol widely deployed on Microsoft Windows machines. The cifs-utils packages contain tools for mounting shares on Linux using the SMB/CIFS protocol. The tools in this package work in conjunction with support in the kernel to allow one to mount a SMB/CIFS share onto a client and use it as if it were a standard Linux file system.

### Hackers Exploited CVSS 10.0 Hex.pm RCE Flaw to Compromise 6 Package Registry Servers
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-25T09:06:59Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-124283, CVE-2026-129514, CVE-2026-130411, CVE-2026-133555 | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: An attacker ran operating system commands on Monday on six production servers of Hex.pm, the package registry that Elixir and Erlang developers fetch their dependencies from. The flaw used in the attack had been in the registry's code since February 2016, and the team that maintains the service had every server on a patched build within about 15 minutes of the first malicious request. Checks
- Technical details: Attackers exploited a decade-old Hex.pm flaw to execute commands on six servers, with potential exposure of account data and private packages.

An attacker ran operating system commands on Monday on six production servers of Hex.pm , the package registry that Elixir and Erlang developers fetch their dependencies from.

The flaw used in the attack had been in the registry's code since February 2016, and the team that maintains the service had every server on a patched build within about 15 minutes of the first malicious request.

Checks since then found no sign that any package was changed. Whether the attacker read account data such as email addresses, password hashes, and two-factor authentication secrets could not be ruled out, the team said.

Everyone with a hex.pm account has been logged out because the maintainers replaced the keys that sign sessions and access tokens. They advise account holders to take these steps:

Reset two-factor authentication by setting up a new authenticator entry, and store the new recovery codes.

Log back in to the client with mix hex.user auth or, for Erlang projects, rebar3 hex user auth.

Update the Hex client when the next release ships, expected within days, with a new registry signing system.

Watch for phishing. Exposed email addresses may be misused, so check that links in any email about Hex go to hex.pm.

API keys do not need to be replaced, according to the team: they are random 128-bit values stored only as HMAC-SHA256 hashes, which it says cannot realistically be brute-forced. Passwords are hashed with bcrypt, which slows guessing but does not stop it for weak passwords, so changing them still matters.

Developers who never created a hex.pm account and only fetch public packages have nothing to reset, Peter Ullrich, one of the report's authors, said in a maintainer clarification on the Elixir Forum. Those who signed up with GitHub should still log back in to their clients, and reset two-factor authentication if they enabled it.

Until the new client ships, the advice is to fetch packages only from repo.hex.pm and to avoid third-party mirrors. The registry signing key has not yet been replaced, and it was among the credentials the attacker could have read.

The flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-129514 with the maximum CVSS score of 10.0, sat in the parser hex.pm used to read request bodies sent in an Erlang data format. That parser ran before routing and authentication, so an unauthenticated POST request to any address on the site could reach it.

The decoding function was meant to be safe, and it did block dangerous terms such as functions, but it accepted maps with a __struct__ key, which allowed a request body to pose as any Elixir struct the application had loaded.

The safety check that would have stopped this was removed from the code on February 23, 2016, which is where the flaw's age comes from. Exploitation before Monday has not been established.

A crafted struct ran nothing by itself. It caused an error, and the error reporting code then inspected the struct. That inspection, via the Ecto and DBConnection database libraries, led to a point where the server called a function of the attacker's choice, which then ran operating system commands with the permissions of the registry's service account.

The security advisory lists hex.pm as affected from February 23, 2016 until Monday. The fix commit makes the decoder reject any term containing a map with a __struct__ key, and every hex.pm server was running it by 18:31 UTC on Monday.

Load balancer logs recorded 69 exploit requests between 18:16 and 18:29 UTC on Monday, all from two addresses, the Hex.pm team said in its incident report yesterday. Files created by the exploit appeared on six web servers, in one case just 3 milliseconds after the request. Three of those servers joined mid-attack, when a restart brought up machines still on the vulnerable build.

To check the packages, the team compared every database table against a copy taken two minutes before the first exploit request and traced each difference to normal activity. The 23 releases published during the attack all match the audit log, no older release was changed, and the package and registry files written that evening match the database records.
- Fix / remediation: Ash , upgrade to 3.38.0 or later ( CVE-2026-124283 , same class of vulnerability). db_connection, upgrade to 2.10.3 or later ( CVE-2026-133555 ) flop , upgrade to the latest version ( CVE-2026-130411 )

### Microsoft confirms authentication issues after November updates
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-25T09:02:57Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-118737 | Topic signals: identity
- Summary: Microsoft has confirmed that customers may experience authentication issues after installing November security updates that address a high-severity remote code execution vulnerability. [...]
- Technical details: Microsoft has confirmed that customers may experience authentication issues after installing November security updates that address a high-severity remote code execution vulnerability.

Tracked as CVE-2026-118737 , this security flaw was found in the Windows Remote Procedure Call Runtime and stems from a timing-dependent authentication weakness that unauthenticated attackers can exploit to remotely execute code without user interaction on unpatched systems.

Microsoft says the November security updates represent the first phase of NTLM message-signing improvements, which reduce exposure to relay attacks by ensuring authenticated NTLM sessions always establish a cryptographic session key and negotiate real message signing.

However, as Microsoft explained in an updated support document, the new security improvements introduced to address this vulnerability may also cause authentication failures on patched devices.

"Microsoft is aware of compatibility issues affecting some third-party apps and devices, including authentication proxy servers, single sign-on (SSO) solutions, and application packaging solutions that use NTLM authentication," it said.

"This issue can occur when such a proxy device does not correctly implement the NTLM protocol. For example, an affected device might set the NTLMSSP_NEGOTIATE_TARGET_INFO flag in its CHALLENGE_MESSAGE but provide an empty or incomplete TargetInfo field."

While a fix is not yet available, Microsoft said affected apps and devices return an NTLMSSP_CHALLENGE message with an empty "Target Info List" in network trace signatures or Target Machine fields with "Not Found" values in event logs.

As a temporary workaround, the company advised customers to go through the following procedure on affected systems:

Set the ClientAllowUnsignedNtlm REG_DWORD registry value under HKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\Lsa\MSV1_0 to 1 (the value used for clients that do not offer SIGN)

Check whether the affected device or application supports a configuration that uses Kerberos authentication or whether the vendor provides a software or firmware update compatible with the NTLM signing requirements introduced by CVE-2026-118737 .

If neither option is available, contact the device or software vendor to request an update that complies with the NTLM protocol requirements described in 3.1.5.1.2 Client Receives a CHALLENGE_MESSAGE from the Server .

However, Microsoft said the ClientAllowUnsignedNtlm registry setting is a short-term solution and may be removed in the future.

"Updating the affected product or using a compatible authentication configuration is the recommended long-term resolution," it noted.

Since it addressed installation issues affecting the November 2026 cumulative updates, Microsoft has also confirmed that this month's security updates are causing Windows activation failures and SQL Server performance issues .

The company is also investigating a known issue that may break printing documents with complex formatting for customers who have installed the latest Windows updates.

- Technical details: An update for raptor2 is now available for Red Hat Enterprise Linux 9.6 Extended Update Support.

Red Hat Product Security has rated this update as having a security impact of Important. A Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) base score, which gives a detailed severity rating, is available for each vulnerability from the CVE link(s) in the References section.

Raptor is the RDF Parser Toolkit for Redland that provides a set of standalone RDF parsers, generating triples from RDF/XML or N-Triples.

raptor2: raptor2: arbitrary code execution via heap-based buffer overflow in language tag handling (CVE-2025-71436)

For more details about the security issue(s), including the impact, a CVSS score, acknowledgments, and other related information, refer to the CVE page(s) listed in the References section.

For details on how to apply this update, which includes the changes described in this advisory, refer to:
