### CrowdStrike agrees to buy Zscaler for $43.9 billion in network security expansion
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-23T16:15:50Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The deal would bring device protection and network access controls under one roof as CrowdStrike seeks a larger role in how companies secure AI agents. The post CrowdStrike agrees to buy Zscaler for $43.9 billion in network security expansion appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: CrowdStrike agreed to acquire Zscaler in a $43.9 billion all-stock deal, combining endpoint and zero-trust network security to protect AI agents.

CrowdStrike said Monday it agreed to acquire Zscaler in an all-stock deal valued at about $43.9 billion, giving the cybersecurity company a network security business alongside its software for protecting devices and cloud applications.

The acquisition would bring two kinds of security under one owner: tools that monitor what happens on a device or in a cloud application, and tools that control what those systems can connect to. That distinction matters as employees, applications and artificial intelligence agents connect directly to cloud services without necessarily routing traffic through a company data center. Zscaler’s approach, known as zero trust, checks whether a user or program is authorized to access a specific resource rather than trusting it based on where it connects from.

“The network used to connect people to places. Now it connects agents to data,” CrowdStrike founder and CEO George Kurtz said in the announcement.

Daniel Bernard, CrowdStrike’s chief business officer, said in an interview that “this isn’t a shotgun wedding” and the companies’ years of working together gave them a view of how customers were already using their products to secure AI agents.

“The network really starts on the endpoint, and that’s the genesis of how we view this transaction,” Bernard said. He said CrowdStrike’s software focuses on what an agent does on the device where it runs, while Zscaler’s software sets rules for what the agent can access over the network.

“But now where the agent travels needs to be governed and controlled as well,” he said. Under one owner, CrowdStrike plans to combine device activity and network records so security teams can examine what happened on a device alongside the connections it made. It also plans to sell both sets of products under a single contract, giving it a way to reach about 8,000 Zscaler customers that do not currently buy from CrowdStrike. “We want to be the operating system of security,” Bernard said. He described a platform where security teams could monitor activity and enforce rules across devices, cloud applications and networks, with the goal of providing “consistency and ease of use in managing security.”

Bernard said other software makers would still be able to build tools that work with CrowdStrike’s platform. “This isn’t a play of saying, ‘Hey, the only vendor yo

### Dutch privacy authority urges consumers not to buy ‘pervert glasses’
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: TechCrunch Security | Published: 2026-11-23T15:29:49Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The Netherlands' data protection agency said smart wearables violate people's privacy, get information wrong, and can result in unsafe situations.
- Business background / implications: The Netherlands' data protection agency said smart wearables violate people's privacy, get information wrong, and can result in unsafe situations.

The Dutch Data Protection Authority is urging consumers not to buy AI-enabled camera glasses, warning that they compromise the privacy of nearby bystanders and risk the safety of their users.

The authority, whose recent statement falls just short of an outright ban, said that use of the internet-enabled and always-on glasses to record people in public is “almost always prohibited.”

Critics have dubbed these smart wearables as “pervert glasses” for their propensity to be used to video people without their permission, particularly to harass women. While some glasses have warning lights when users are recording, privacy advocates have argued that these alerts are not clear enough. Some people have even made phone apps capable of detecting nearby smart glasses wearers.

The Dutch data protection authority warned that the glasses also produce wrong results and unsafe results. In one test carried out by the Dutch authority, a pair of unnamed glasses “gave dating advice to an adult who indicated that they are interested in a minor.” The glasses also incorrectly read a medicine label and claimed to have called an ambulance when it had not.

The Dutch government’s advice follows a similar move by the Norwegian government , which in September said it was considering a ban on camera-enabled devices.

### I’ve never hunted for a software vulnerability before. AI helped me find one in six hours.
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-23T11:00:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: With help from two free AI models, I found and reported a real flaw in a WordPress plugin used by more than 40,000 sites. The unnerving part wasn’t just that it worked — it was how little experience I needed to get there. The post I’ve never hunted for a software vulnerability before. AI helped me find one in six hours. appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: A CyberScoop reporter with no coding experience used two free AI models to uncover a critical WPIDE WordPress flaw (CVE-2026-124251) in six hours.

It came to me after listening to Michael Aiello, OpenAI’s cyber executive in residence, talk about the AI slop overwhelming open-source maintainers with vulnerability disclosure. I found there was an underlying message throughout his remarks: It was so remarkably easy for people to find vulnerabilities with AI-assisted cybersecurity tools. To drive it home, he at one point referred to AI agents as having the mindset of “a BMX biker gang made up entirely of 12-year-olds.”

The joke set off a light bulb in my head: If this guy is talking about this technology this way, how hard could it be to find vulnerabilities? Could I find one?

Turns out the answer is yes, I could. And the process by which I found one was easier than I could have imagined.

Over the course of six hours and the use of two freely available LLMs, I managed to discover, test, and submit a vulnerability report for a WordPress plugin that has over 40,000 active installations. The vulnerability — a cross-site request forgery — was validated 24 hours after submission to a third party , and assigned a CVE entry: CVE-2026-124251 .

The following is a diary of this discovery, which I believe can provide some perspective to the current landscape of AI-assisted vulnerability discovery.

While I doubt this bug will become the next php2shell , I do believe it adds perspective on what so many experts have been discussing over the course of this year: For many classes of bugs, the barrier for discovery has disappeared. While I have an elemental grasp on how this industry works, I am a reporter who is better with words than code. Yet, with only my MacBook and some time to kill, I surfaced a real flaw in software running on tens of thousands of sites.

If I am finding vulnerabilities this way, then thousands of people are discovering that they can do this, too. Some of them are curious. Some of them are paid. Some of them mean harm. The technical report of the vulnerability can be found here .

While the process was easy, I knew it wasn’t going to be as simple as firing up a large language model and entering “Find a software vulnerability on the internet. Make no mistakes.” But where an LLM did initially help me (Gemini Flash 3.8, to be precise) was determining how my introductory effort at finding a vulnerability would yield the best results without breaking

### Signal restricts mobile screenshots as it confronts ‘existential threat’ of AI surveillance
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: TechCrunch Security | Published: 2026-11-21T21:37:10Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Signal is limiting users' ability to take screenshots in both its Android and iOS apps.
- Business background / implications: Signal is limiting users' ability to take screenshots in both its Android and iOS apps.

Signal announced Friday that it’s limiting users’ ability to take screenshots within the secure messaging app — a response to what president Meredith Whittaker described as the “existential threat” of operating systems that integrate agentic AI.

Whittaker wrote that by giving AI agents the ability to “scan” the information on users’ screens, Windows, Android, and macOS/iOS “are undermining Signal’s ability to independently guarantee privacy.”

“Now, in service of AI agentic capabilities, your sensitive data is being captured, stored, and made accessible to whatever local database or remote server an OS-level AI agent is using at that moment,” she said. “All of this is outside of Signal’s control once the data has been captured, and imperils the privacy of all the participants in a conversation.”

Signal already sought to limit these risks by blocking screenshots of its Windows app by default. Now it’s adding similar restrictions in Android and iOS.

That said, users can disable this setting if they wish, which Whittaker said would “break our privacy guarantees.” She also admitted this is an “incomplete” solution, as it doesn’t “address other more sneaky ways AI agents could potentially access application data.”

### Trump says reported FBI database hack is ‘not good’
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: TechCrunch Security | Published: 2026-11-21T18:44:53Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The FBI is reportedly investigating a hack of the National Crime Information Center.
- Business background / implications: The FBI is reportedly investigating a hack of the National Crime Information Center.

The FBI is investigating an apparent hack of the National Crime Information Center (NCIC), according to reports in CNN and the Associated Press .

The NCIC is the online database used by federal and local law enforcement to share information about warrants, background checks, and more. The scope of the breach does not yet appear to be clear.

A former FBI official told CNN that they’re not just concerned about “whether hackers gained access to sensitive information, although that is an issue, but whether they could compromise the accuracy of the information that officers rely on.”

President Donald Trump addressed the reports during an interview Saturday with Fox News, saying, “We just heard about it and I will say that it’s not good.” At the same time, Trump also said that as a result of the breach, “maybe we’ll learn things that we would have never learned otherwise because, you know, you had the Russia, Russia, Russia hoax.”

In September, the FBI reportedly told agents and support staff that their personal information had been stolen in a cyberattack .

### Experts warn: Nearly everyone in AI is susceptible to a honey-trap scheme right now
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: TechCrunch Security | Published: 2026-11-20T20:58:29Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Techies are building technology that can advance all aspects of the world — but can they protect their hearts? That's the vulnerability that honey pots exploit.
- Business background / implications: Techies are building technology that can advance all aspects of the world — but can they protect their hearts? That's the vulnerability that honey pots exploit.

Even as the fall-out for Nvidia over Jensen Huang’s alleged honey-trap scandal is still unfolding, Silicon Valley has yet to reckon with a new reality: many more people among them are at risk.

“Nobody briefs founders on counterintelligence,” Aaron Brown, a former senior CIA officer and current founder of the software company Lumbra, told TechCrunch.

That’s because historically honey traps have been associated with politicians, the military, and national intelligence — the people “behind clearances and a workforce trained to expect an approach,” from spies and other attackers.

But now many of the most valuable secrets “sit inside companies building chips, models, and infrastructure,” Brown says, adding that a spy “wants what the CEO can touch. The roadmap, the export decisions, the customer list, who gets the technology. The CEO is the shortest path to all of it.”

Huang has been accused of falling for just such a honey trap by having an affair with Alice Huang. She’s a former executive of a Singapore neocloud who’s been accused of being a Chinese spy and of smuggling billions of dollars’ worth of restricted Nvidia chips into China.

After the Huang allegations broke, jokes flooded the internet about which tech CEO will be next, but it’s not just CEOs and founders who are at risk. The WSJ reported that OpenAI started warning its employees about outsiders trying to cozy up to them in search of company secrets. Anyone with access to sensitive information — from board members to executive assistants — can be the target of a honey trap.

And typically, the attackers aren’t lovers. Friendship is actually the oldest honey-trap scheme around, Brown said.

Honey traps gain access to their targets as investors, customers, recruiters, and consultancies. Their requests look legitimate on paper and don’t need their targets to fall in love. “They need you to feel comfortable talking,” Brown said. “They last longer because nobody’s looking.”

What’s interesting, too, is the irony. So many tech founders are moving to automate and digitize so much of human life and yet remain vulnerable to the oldest bug around, a person who preys on the human need to be understood by others.

“Why fight through a firewall when you can get close to the person who can open doors, influence decisions, or make requests nobody want
- Action: Not applicable as a vulnerability fix; verify deal stage and material claims against company announcements or filings.

### A transcription firm used by police, prosecutors, and tech giants exposed customer data online
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: TechCrunch Security | Published: 2026-11-20T15:46:07Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Exclusive: A simple security lapse exposed transcriptions of police interviews and bodycam footage on a cloud server without a password.
- Business background / implications: Exclusive: A simple security lapse exposed transcriptions of police interviews and bodycam footage on a cloud server without a password.

A transcription company that caters to law enforcement, government departments, and Silicon Valley tech giants has resolved a security lapse after leaving a large amount of internal data, including customer transcriptions and their source materials, exposed to the web.

The data security lapse at San Francisco-based Tigerfish exposed transcriptions of police interviews, body-cam footage, and customers’ raw audio and video footage. The exposed data also included invoices of the company’s customers, which include police departments and prosecutors’ offices.

According to Anurag Sen , a good faith security researcher who found the data, the files were located on an Amazon-hosted (S3) cloud server that was openly listed and accessible to anyone via an easy-to-guess web address — no passwords needed.

The security lapse underscores how simple flaws or misconfigurations can result in sensitive information getting exposed online, especially as AI makes it easier to uncover weaknesses that can put companies and their data at risk.

Sen discovered the exposed data online in October and alerted the company to the issue, but did not hear back. Sen later provided details to TechCrunch in an effort to flag the incident with the company for resolution.

Sen, 30, is no stranger to TechCrunch. For the past decade, he has searched for sensitive datasets exposed online with the goal of notifying the companies and organizations that their information is open to the internet. Like many security researchers, much of his efforts are driven by a duty to secure the internet, and he describes his work as an “unpaid air-traffic controller.”

From his island home in Indonesia, Sen has uncovered sensitive U.S. military emails spilling from a compartmentalized Department of Defense cloud server; databases exposing reams of customer records ; the inner workings of a country’s national license plate surveillance system ; and public systems that inadvertently sent out streams of SMS codes used by people around the world to log into their online accounts.

Given Sen’s propensity for finding often highly confidential information online, we verified the source of the leak and the data, and contacted Tigerfish.

Soon after making contact, Tigerfish secured its exposed S3 cloud bucket.

“We’ve blocked all public access to the bucket you identified, includin

### The US national cyber director is stepping down
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: TechCrunch Security | Published: 2026-11-20T09:14:19Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The news comes as businesses across the country report a flurry of technical outages, the causes of which are still unknown.
- Business background / implications: The news comes as businesses across the country report a flurry of technical outages, the causes of which are still unknown.

White House national cyber director Sean Cairncross will be stepping down from his role, Axios reported. The news comes as Democrats prepare to take control of Congress and as businesses across the country report a flurry of technical outages whose cause remains unknown.

Cairncross, the president’s principal adviser on national cybersecurity policy and strategy, took office in August 2025. Since then, he has met with top AI leaders and helped the Trump administration make key AI decisions, according to Axios. The outlet also reports that the split between Cairncross and the White House was amicable, and his departure was timed so that the White House could nab a Senate confirmation for his role before Democrats take control of Congress in January.

Cairncross, a lawyer and longtime Republican operative, had no cybersecurity background when the Senate confirmed him 59–35. He previously served as the Republican National Committee’s chief operating officer and, during Trump’s first term, as CEO of the Millennium Challenge Corporation, a federal foreign aid agency. The office hasn’t been around long; Congress created it in early 2021, and Cairncross was only its third Senate-confirmed director. The first director, Chris Inglis, left after about 19 months, and the job then went about 10 months without a permanent director.

### These tech outages are giving Y2K, AI agent style
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: TechCrunch Security | Published: 2026-11-19T21:42:31Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Are the recent outages in the airline booking systems, banking platforms, and productivity platforms really a coincidence? There's actually likely something deeper going on, cybersecurity experts say.
- Business background / implications: Are the recent outages in the airline booking systems, banking platforms, and productivity platforms really a coincidence? There's actually likely something deeper going on, cybersecurity experts say.

It’s going down, as the American rapper Yung Joc might say: the airline booking systems, banking platforms, and productivity platforms. In the past two weeks, the U.S.’s top businesses have faced technical outages that have disrupted the systems that run everyday life.

Apple Pay went down. NASA’s internal communication network went down. The FAA had to ground all flights for hours (only the third time in history since 9/11), and Microsoft was hit with an outage that took down Outlook, Teams, and Copilot for nearly two days.

The first thought, naturally, is an AI hacker invasion of some sort, especially after hackers managed to attack fridges in a grocery store and a blood bank in France a few weeks ago. Here in the U.S., the director of National Intelligence said these outages don’t seem related to state-sponsored terrorism, while President Trump called the issues “individual events.” Many of the companies themselves have said the outages were not caused by cyberattacks or AI attacks, but tech issues.

Only, there’s the fact that they all struck within a short timespan of each other, and just a few weeks after Cybersecurity’s so-called “Black October,” when a slew of also-unrelated cybersecurity incidents occurred on October 21, striking PHP, Node.js, LiteLLM, more.

While there’s no reason to view these outages as cyberattacks, there may be more than coincidence going on: It’s giving AI-native Y2K, frankly.

Dave DeWalt, an investor who worked in cybersecurity for more than three decades (including as president and CEO of McAfee and as vice chairman of the CISA Advisory Council for the Department of Homeland Security), also thinks it’s probably AI-related.

The technical outages are most likely the “growing pains of a security system that’s getting stronger, not weaker,” and that “AI is surfacing vulnerabilities at a speed and scale we’ve never had before through AI tools like Mythos and others,” he told TechCrunch.

The thing is, many of these technical flaws have always existed, but because AI is surfacing them so fast, enterprises are rushing to fix them before bad actors can exploit them. “That surge in patching is disrupting things in the short term,” he said.

This was, perhaps, the first moment when legacy meets modern, Anusha Iyer, a software engi

## Analyst workflow

1. Match KEV/advisories to asset inventory, versions and internet exposure.
2. Verify exploit status, affected versions and remediation against primary vendor/CERT advisories.
3. Derive behavior hunts from documented TTPs; map ATT&CK only when supported by evidence.
4. Verify candidate IOCs against original source, freshness and local telemetry.
5. Keep queries Draft until schema, syntax, positive/negative tests and tuning are recorded.

## Limitations

- Broad public-source baseline, not exhaustive global intelligence.
- ThreatFox, OTX, GreyNoise and Shadowserver are not enabled; review credentials and provider terms before integration.
- Business discovery uses a wider 7-day window; Google News results are reported leads and require company-announcement or filing verification.
- Feed failures and quiet periods do not mean absence of threats; report timestamps are IST and machine timestamps in JSON are UTC.
